I’ve been saying yes to too many things—projects, workshops, fixing up the studio, client calls—and at first it felt productive, you know? Like I was finally catching up on everything.\n\nBut now I’m looking at all these deadlines and the mortgage payment, and it’s like… I may have taken on more than I can actually manage. My studio is full of half-started improvements, which reminds me, I found these old canvases I’d forgotten about—anyway, sorry. The main thing is I’m overwhelmed by how much I’ve committed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about not being able to meet all these deadlines and commitments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… anxious is probably the word, yes. It’s like I said yes to too many projects and workshops and all these client calls, and then I started tearing into the studio improvements too—because I thought, well, if I’m already reorganizing, I might as well fix the lighting, and then the shelves, and then—\n\nBut now the deadlines are all sort of stacked up, and the mortgage payment is sitting there in the back of my mind. So yes, I’m worried I can’t realistically keep up with everything I’ve taken on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say avoiding, exactly. I mean, I’ve been putting off some things, but it’s more like I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like the studio shelves, and then I find old sketchbooks, and then suddenly it’s two hours later.\n\nPlaces… no, not really. I haven’t noticed myself avoiding places because of it. Activities maybe I delay, but I’m not sure it’s the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to finish one task before moving on to another?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I mean, in theory, yes, I like the idea of finishing something before moving on, but in practice I don’t really do that. I’ll start with one thing, like answering an email, and then I notice a supply invoice, and then that reminds me the studio sink still needs fixing, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in that corner is too cold for photographing work.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have to finish one task first. It’s more like I keep getting pulled sideways into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel driven to, if that makes sense. Not exactly “have to” like someone is making me, but there’s this current in me from the morning, like a hum, and sitting still feels almost impossible.\n\nSo I’ll start rearranging the studio, then I’m drafting a proposal, then I’m emailing three people, then I’m messing with supplies because the brushes are in the wrong place—or they seem like they are—and then I remember I bought those little drawer labels years ago and never used them. It feels like I want to, but also like I can’t really leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or concerns over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same worries, exactly. I do circle back to things, but it’s usually because one thing reminds me of another. Like I’ll think about an email I haven’t answered, and then that makes me think of a frame order, and then I’m wondering whether I should move that stack of paper away from the window because of the light.\n\nSo… over and over on one worry? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way. It feels more like my mind keeps branching off, not looping on one single concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you feel overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exactly. I mean, sometimes I feel sort of keyed up, but I don’t usually stop and check, like, “Is my heart racing?” or anything.\n\nI might be moving around a lot, picking things up, putting them down, going from the table to the shelves—actually the shelves are a whole thing, because I keep meaning to reorganize them by paper size and then I find something I forgot I had. But sweating or trembling… I’m not sure. Nothing really stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to function in your daily life because of these feelings of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but things are slipping. My illustration deadlines, for one — I keep thinking I can catch up in one big push, and then I take on another thing, or decide the whole setup needs changing first.\n\nPeople I work with have started checking in on me, which is embarrassing. Not in a mean way, they’re kind, but it’s like, “Are you still on track?” and I hate that they have to ask.\n\nAnd the money part is… that’s harder to say. I overcommitted, and I spent money I really should’ve kept aside for the studio, and now the mortgage is at risk. I keep thinking if I finish everything quickly it’ll balance out, but right now, yes, it’s affecting daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is. I keep trying to talk myself out of calling it distress, like it’s just a crowded season or the usual studio mess, but it’s more than that.\n\nMy illustration deadlines have slipped, and people I work with have had to check in on me, which makes me feel awful—like I’m being watched, or like I’ve become unreliable. And then I start thinking about the studio and the supplies and how I was sure certain purchases would pay off because I’d finish everything quickly, but now the mortgage is at risk because I overcommitted and spent money I should’ve kept aside.\n\nSo yes, I’m distressed. I’m trying not to panic about it, but it’s sitting there all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you've been avoiding lately because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one specific thing. It’s more like I circle around tasks… I’ll open the email, then think, no, I should clean the table first so I can think properly, and then I find a stack of old sketches and suddenly I’m sorting those.\n\nSo maybe I’m avoiding things, but I don’t always recognize it as avoiding in the moment. It just feels like everything has a step before it, and then another step before that. But a clear list of “I’m avoiding this exact task”… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your worries or concerns even when you're trying to relax or focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. Sometimes I think I’m relaxing, but then I realize I’ve been staring at the same cup of tea or the same page for ten minutes.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s “worrying” exactly, or just my mind jumping around. Like I’ll sit down to watch something, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in the studio is wrong, or how I should reorganize the brushes—actually, I used to have them in these old jam jars, which looked nice but tipped over constantly, and I kept meaning to fix that…\n\nSorry. I guess I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say. It’s hard for me to separate relaxing from just… drifting into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or concerns when you're trying to fall asleep or relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s the same thought exactly. At night I’ll start thinking I’m just going to lie down and breathe, and then suddenly I’m remembering that I didn’t rinse a brush properly, or there was this one set of inks I bought years ago that leaked in a drawer—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking whether it repeats. It feels more like things drift into each other when I’m trying to settle, and then I’m awake longer than I meant to be. But I’m not sure I could name one concern that keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Forcing” sounds very strong. Sometimes a thought kind of barges in, but then again, that might just be how thinking works? Like I’ll be trying to read or make dinner and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished in the studio, and then that reminds me of a painting I never varnished, and then I’m off somewhere else entirely.\n\nBut I don’t know if it feels forced exactly. It’s more like my mind changes rooms without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that you cannot control or that keep coming back even when you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes something comes back, but I don’t know if I’m “pushing it away” so much as getting distracted from it and then finding it again.\n\nLike if I think, oh, I need to deal with that stack of paper, and then I’m suddenly remembering a frame I bought that never fit anything—actually it was a beautiful frame, too ornate, I should’ve measured first—and then later the paper thing comes back. But that feels more like loose threads than something I can’t control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a particular thought that keeps returning no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently putting off tasks that you know you need to do?\n\nPatient: I guess I do sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “consistently.” There are things I mean to get to, like sorting supplies or answering an email, and then I’ll notice some other little thing that needs doing first, and somehow I’m cleaning a palette knife from two days ago or looking for a receipt for something completely unrelated.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I put things off, but it doesn’t always feel like I’m avoiding them on purpose. It just sort of gets… sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a bit. Mostly because I’ve been feeling so unusually… buoyant, I guess? Like everything finally makes sense—the studio, the garden, all these plans clicking into place—and I get very excited about it.\n\nBut then if someone asks practical questions, like about invoices or whether I’ve thought a plan through, I can get sharp with them. Sharper than I mean to. My sister asked about paperwork the other day and I just snapped, which wasn’t fair. It’s like I’m lit up and moving, and anyone questioning it feels like they’re putting a handbrake on something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work or significantly reduce your work or social activities because of your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to say “not exactly time off,” because I still go into the studio, I still move things around and start things. But the actual illustration deadlines have slipped. A couple of colleagues have had to check in on me, which was embarrassing—though one of them has always been very calendar-minded, she even color-codes things, which I used to think was excessive, but maybe it isn’t.\n\nAnd socially… I’m not sure I’ve reduced it so much as made everything more complicated. I overcommitted to things, and I spent money I really should have kept back for the studio. Now the mortgage is… not in a comfortable place. It’s at risk, I suppose. I hate saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to maintain relationships or complete tasks at work due to your current feelings of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the clearest place it shows up, really. The illustration work is where I can usually keep myself together—well, “usually” meaning I know the rhythms of it, the paper, the deadlines, the little rituals with sharpening pencils, which sounds silly but it helps.\n\nBut lately the actual deadlines have slipped. Not just “I’ll send it tomorrow” slipped, more like people are having to check in on me, colleagues asking where things are, and I hate that. It makes me feel like I’ve become unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve overcommitted to things and spent money I should have kept for the studio. I kept thinking it would all come together somehow, but now the mortgage is at risk, which is… frightening. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting things in a real way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I haven’t had voices or visions outside of this period.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My illustration deadlines have slipped, colleagues have had to check in on me, and the mortgage is now at risk because I overcommitted and spent money I needed to keep the studio secure.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three months, since I took on the mortgage for the cottage-studio.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / artist_or_related_worker / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a bit. Mostly because I’ve been feeling so unusually… buoyant, I guess? Like everything finally makes sense—the studio, the garden, all these plans clicking into place—and I get very excited about it.\n\nBut then if someone asks practical questions, like about invoices or whether I’ve thought a plan through, I can get sharp with them. Sharper than I mean to. My sister asked about paperwork the other day and I just snapped, which wasn’t fair. It’s like I’m lit up and moving, and anyone questioning it feels like they’re putting a handbrake on something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work or significantly reduce your work or social activities because of your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to say “not exactly time off,” because I still go into the studio, I still move things around and start things. But the actual illustration deadlines have slipped. A couple of colleagues have had to check in on me, which was embarrassing—though one of them has always been very calendar-minded, she even color-codes things, which I used to think was excessive, but maybe it isn’t.\n\nAnd socially… I’m not sure I’ve reduced it so much as made everything more complicated. I overcommitted to things, and I spent money I really should have kept back for the studio. Now the mortgage is… not in a comfortable place. It’s at risk, I suppose. I hate saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to maintain relationships or complete tasks at work due to your current feelings of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the clearest place it shows up, really. The illustration work is where I can usually keep myself together—well, “usually” meaning I know the rhythms of it, the paper, the deadlines, the little rituals with sharpening pencils, which sounds silly but it helps.\n\nBut lately the actual deadlines have slipped. Not just “I’ll send it tomorrow” slipped, more like people are having to check in on me, colleagues asking where things are, and I hate that. It makes me feel like I’ve become unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve overcommitted to things and spent money I should have kept for the studio. I kept thinking it would all come together somehow, but now the mortgage is at risk, which is… frightening. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting things in a real way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself regularly avoiding certain tasks or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t know if I’d say I avoid them regularly… not in a tidy way, anyway. I can get sort of… sidetracked from things that feel unpleasant, like forms or phone calls, but then sometimes I’ll decide I’m absolutely going to reorganize an entire shelf of inks instead, because that feels more “useful,” even if it’s not the thing I was meant to do.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because things have gotten a bit out of hand. I’ve been saying yes to too many things—projects, workshops, fixing up the studio, client calls—and at first it felt productive, you know? Like I was finally catching up on everything.\n\nBut now I’m looking at all these deadlines and the mortgage payment, and it’s like… I may have taken on more than I can actually manage. My studio is full of half-started improvements, which reminds me, I found these old canvases I’d forgotten about—anyway, sorry. The main thing is I’m overwhelmed by how much I’ve committed to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious about not being able to meet all these deadlines and commitments?\n\nPatient: Yes, I mean… anxious is probably the word, yes. It’s like I said yes to too many projects and workshops and all these client calls, and then I started tearing into the studio improvements too—because I thought, well, if I’m already reorganizing, I might as well fix the lighting, and then the shelves, and then—\n\nBut now the deadlines are all sort of stacked up, and the mortgage payment is sitting there in the back of my mind. So yes, I’m worried I can’t realistically keep up with everything I’ve taken on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or places because of feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say avoiding, exactly. I mean, I’ve been putting off some things, but it’s more like I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like the studio shelves, and then I find old sketchbooks, and then suddenly it’s two hours later.\n\nPlaces… no, not really. I haven’t noticed myself avoiding places because of it. Activities maybe I delay, but I’m not sure it’s the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to finish one task before moving on to another?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I mean, in theory, yes, I like the idea of finishing something before moving on, but in practice I don’t really do that. I’ll start with one thing, like answering an email, and then I notice a supply invoice, and then that reminds me the studio sink still needs fixing, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in that corner is too cold for photographing work.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have to finish one task first. It’s more like I keep getting pulled sideways into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these tasks because you have to, or because you want to?\n\nPatient: I think… more because I feel driven to, if that makes sense. Not exactly “have to” like someone is making me, but there’s this current in me from the morning, like a hum, and sitting still feels almost impossible.\n\nSo I’ll start rearranging the studio, then I’m drafting a proposal, then I’m emailing three people, then I’m messing with supplies because the brushes are in the wrong place—or they seem like they are—and then I remember I bought those little drawer labels years ago and never used them. It feels like I want to, but also like I can’t really leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or concerns over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same worries, exactly. I do circle back to things, but it’s usually because one thing reminds me of another. Like I’ll think about an email I haven’t answered, and then that makes me think of a frame order, and then I’m wondering whether I should move that stack of paper away from the window because of the light.\n\nSo… over and over on one worry? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that way. It feels more like my mind keeps branching off, not looping on one single concern.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you feel overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that exactly. I mean, sometimes I feel sort of keyed up, but I don’t usually stop and check, like, “Is my heart racing?” or anything.\n\nI might be moving around a lot, picking things up, putting them down, going from the table to the shelves—actually the shelves are a whole thing, because I keep meaning to reorganize them by paper size and then I find something I forgot I had. But sweating or trembling… I’m not sure. Nothing really stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to function in your daily life because of these feelings of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but things are slipping. My illustration deadlines, for one — I keep thinking I can catch up in one big push, and then I take on another thing, or decide the whole setup needs changing first.\n\nPeople I work with have started checking in on me, which is embarrassing. Not in a mean way, they’re kind, but it’s like, “Are you still on track?” and I hate that they have to ask.\n\nAnd the money part is… that’s harder to say. I overcommitted, and I spent money I really should’ve kept aside for the studio, and now the mortgage is at risk. I keep thinking if I finish everything quickly it’ll balance out, but right now, yes, it’s affecting daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is. I keep trying to talk myself out of calling it distress, like it’s just a crowded season or the usual studio mess, but it’s more than that.\n\nMy illustration deadlines have slipped, and people I work with have had to check in on me, which makes me feel awful—like I’m being watched, or like I’ve become unreliable. And then I start thinking about the studio and the supplies and how I was sure certain purchases would pay off because I’d finish everything quickly, but now the mortgage is at risk because I overcommitted and spent money I should’ve kept aside.\n\nSo yes, I’m distressed. I’m trying not to panic about it, but it’s sitting there all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you've been avoiding lately because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one specific thing. It’s more like I circle around tasks… I’ll open the email, then think, no, I should clean the table first so I can think properly, and then I find a stack of old sketches and suddenly I’m sorting those.\n\nSo maybe I’m avoiding things, but I don’t always recognize it as avoiding in the moment. It just feels like everything has a step before it, and then another step before that. But a clear list of “I’m avoiding this exact task”… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your worries or concerns even when you're trying to relax or focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. Sometimes I think I’m relaxing, but then I realize I’ve been staring at the same cup of tea or the same page for ten minutes.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s “worrying” exactly, or just my mind jumping around. Like I’ll sit down to watch something, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in the studio is wrong, or how I should reorganize the brushes—actually, I used to have them in these old jam jars, which looked nice but tipped over constantly, and I kept meaning to fix that…\n\nSorry. I guess I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say. It’s hard for me to separate relaxing from just… drifting into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or concerns when you're trying to fall asleep or relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s the same thought exactly. At night I’ll start thinking I’m just going to lie down and breathe, and then suddenly I’m remembering that I didn’t rinse a brush properly, or there was this one set of inks I bought years ago that leaked in a drawer—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking whether it repeats. It feels more like things drift into each other when I’m trying to settle, and then I’m awake longer than I meant to be. But I’m not sure I could name one concern that keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Forcing” sounds very strong. Sometimes a thought kind of barges in, but then again, that might just be how thinking works? Like I’ll be trying to read or make dinner and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished in the studio, and then that reminds me of a painting I never varnished, and then I’m off somewhere else entirely.\n\nBut I don’t know if it feels forced exactly. It’s more like my mind changes rooms without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that you cannot control or that keep coming back even when you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes something comes back, but I don’t know if I’m “pushing it away” so much as getting distracted from it and then finding it again.\n\nLike if I think, oh, I need to deal with that stack of paper, and then I’m suddenly remembering a frame I bought that never fit anything—actually it was a beautiful frame, too ornate, I should’ve measured first—and then later the paper thing comes back. But that feels more like loose threads than something I can’t control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a particular thought that keeps returning no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently putting off tasks that you know you need to do?\n\nPatient: I guess I do sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “consistently.” There are things I mean to get to, like sorting supplies or answering an email, and then I’ll notice some other little thing that needs doing first, and somehow I’m cleaning a palette knife from two days ago or looking for a receipt for something completely unrelated.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I put things off, but it doesn’t always feel like I’m avoiding them on purpose. It just sort of gets… sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a bit. Mostly because I’ve been feeling so unusually… buoyant, I guess? Like everything finally makes sense—the studio, the garden, all these plans clicking into place—and I get very excited about it.\n\nBut then if someone asks practical questions, like about invoices or whether I’ve thought a plan through, I can get sharp with them. Sharper than I mean to. My sister asked about paperwork the other day and I just snapped, which wasn’t fair. It’s like I’m lit up and moving, and anyone questioning it feels like they’re putting a handbrake on something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work or significantly reduce your work or social activities because of your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to say “not exactly time off,” because I still go into the studio, I still move things around and start things. I keep thinking if I finish everything quickly it’ll balance out, but right now, yes, it’s affecting daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it is. I keep trying to talk myself out of calling it distress, like it’s just a crowded season or the usual studio mess, but it’s more than that.\n\nMy illustration deadlines have slipped, and people I work with have had to check in on me, which makes me feel awful—like I’m being watched, or like I’ve become unreliable. And then I start thinking about the studio and the supplies and how I was sure certain purchases would pay off because I’d finish everything quickly, but now the mortgage is at risk because I overcommitted and spent money I should’ve kept aside.\n\nSo yes, I’m distressed. I’m trying not to panic about it, but it’s sitting there all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or tasks that you've been avoiding lately because they feel too overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to one specific thing. It’s more like I circle around tasks… I’ll open the email, then think, no, I should clean the table first so I can think properly, and then I find a stack of old sketches and suddenly I’m sorting those.\n\nSo maybe I’m avoiding things, but I don’t always recognize it as avoiding in the moment. It just feels like everything has a step before it, and then another step before that. But a clear list of “I’m avoiding this exact task”… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your worries or concerns even when you're trying to relax or focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. Sometimes I think I’m relaxing, but then I realize I’ve been staring at the same cup of tea or the same page for ten minutes.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s “worrying” exactly, or just my mind jumping around. Like I’ll sit down to watch something, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in the studio is wrong, or how I should reorganize the brushes—actually, I used to have them in these old jam jars, which looked nice but tipped over constantly, and I kept meaning to fix that…\n\nSorry. I guess I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say. It’s hard for me to separate relaxing from just… drifting into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or concerns when you're trying to fall asleep or relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s the same thought exactly. At night I’ll start thinking I’m just going to lie down and breathe, and then suddenly I’m remembering that I didn’t rinse a brush properly, or there was this one set of inks I bought years ago that leaked in a drawer—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking whether it repeats. It feels more like things drift into each other when I’m trying to settle, and then I’m awake longer than I meant to be. But I’m not sure I could name one concern that keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Forcing” sounds very strong. Sometimes a thought kind of barges in, but then again, that might just be how thinking works? Like I’ll be trying to read or make dinner and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished in the studio, and then that reminds me of a painting I never varnished, and then I’m off somewhere else entirely.\n\nBut I don’t know if it feels forced exactly. It’s more like my mind changes rooms without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that you cannot control or that keep coming back even when you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes something comes back, but I don’t know if I’m “pushing it away” so much as getting distracted from it and then finding it again.\n\nLike if I think, oh, I need to deal with that stack of paper, and then I’m suddenly remembering a frame I bought that never fit anything—actually it was a beautiful frame, too ornate, I should’ve measured first—and then later the paper thing comes back. But that feels more like loose threads than something I can’t control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a particular thought that keeps returning no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently putting off tasks that you know you need to do?\n\nPatient: I guess I do sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “consistently.” There are things I mean to get to, like sorting supplies or answering an email, and then I’ll notice some other little thing that needs doing first, and somehow I’m cleaning a palette knife from two days ago or looking for a receipt for something completely unrelated.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I put things off, but it doesn’t always feel like I’m avoiding them on purpose. It just sort of gets… sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a bit. Mostly because I’ve been feeling so unusually… buoyant, I guess? It just feels like everything has a step before it, and then another step before that. But a clear list of “I’m avoiding this exact task”… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your worries or concerns even when you're trying to relax or focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure. Sometimes I think I’m relaxing, but then I realize I’ve been staring at the same cup of tea or the same page for ten minutes.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s “worrying” exactly, or just my mind jumping around. Like I’ll sit down to watch something, and then I’m thinking about whether the light in the studio is wrong, or how I should reorganize the brushes—actually, I used to have them in these old jam jars, which looked nice but tipped over constantly, and I kept meaning to fix that…\n\nSorry. I guess I haven’t really noticed it clearly enough to say. It’s hard for me to separate relaxing from just… drifting into the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or concerns when you're trying to fall asleep or relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s the same thought exactly. At night I’ll start thinking I’m just going to lie down and breathe, and then suddenly I’m remembering that I didn’t rinse a brush properly, or there was this one set of inks I bought years ago that leaked in a drawer—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been tracking whether it repeats. It feels more like things drift into each other when I’m trying to settle, and then I’m awake longer than I meant to be. But I’m not sure I could name one concern that keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Forcing” sounds very strong. Sometimes a thought kind of barges in, but then again, that might just be how thinking works? Like I’ll be trying to read or make dinner and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished in the studio, and then that reminds me of a painting I never varnished, and then I’m off somewhere else entirely.\n\nBut I don’t know if it feels forced exactly. It’s more like my mind changes rooms without telling me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that you cannot control or that keep coming back even when you try to push them away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes something comes back, but I don’t know if I’m “pushing it away” so much as getting distracted from it and then finding it again.\n\nLike if I think, oh, I need to deal with that stack of paper, and then I’m suddenly remembering a frame I bought that never fit anything—actually it was a beautiful frame, too ornate, I should’ve measured first—and then later the paper thing comes back. But that feels more like loose threads than something I can’t control.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a particular thought that keeps returning no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself consistently putting off tasks that you know you need to do?\n\nPatient: I guess I do sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “consistently.” There are things I mean to get to, like sorting supplies or answering an email, and then I’ll notice some other little thing that needs doing first, and somehow I’m cleaning a palette knife from two days ago or looking for a receipt for something completely unrelated.\n\nSo… yes, maybe I put things off, but it doesn’t always feel like I’m avoiding them on purpose. It just sort of gets… sidetracked.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers due to your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, a bit. Mostly because I’ve been feeling so unusually… buoyant, I guess? Like everything finally makes sense—the studio, the garden, all these plans clicking into place—and I get very excited about it.\n\nBut then if someone asks practical questions, like about invoices or whether I’ve thought a plan through, I can get sharp with them. Sharper than I mean to. My sister asked about paperwork the other day and I just snapped, which wasn’t fair. It’s like I’m lit up and moving, and anyone questioning it feels like they’re putting a handbrake on something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work or significantly reduce your work or social activities because of your current situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to say “not exactly time off,” because I still go into the studio, I still move things around and start things. But the actual illustration deadlines have slipped. A couple of colleagues have had to check in on me, which was embarrassing—though one of them has always been very calendar-minded, she even color-codes things, which I used to think was excessive, but maybe it isn’t.\n\nAnd socially… I’m not sure I’ve reduced it so much as made everything more complicated. I overcommitted to things, and I spent money I really should have kept back for the studio. Now the mortgage is… not in a comfortable place. It’s at risk, I suppose. I hate saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to maintain relationships or complete tasks at work due to your current feelings of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the clearest place it shows up, really. The illustration work is where I can usually keep myself together—well, “usually” meaning I know the rhythms of it, the paper, the deadlines, the little rituals with sharpening pencils, which sounds silly but it helps.\n\nBut lately the actual deadlines have slipped. Not just “I’ll send it tomorrow” slipped, more like people are having to check in on me, colleagues asking where things are, and I hate that. It makes me feel like I’ve become unreliable.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’ve overcommitted to things and spent money I should have kept for the studio. I kept thinking it would all come together somehow, but now the mortgage is at risk, which is… frightening. I don’t like admitting that, but yes, it’s affecting things in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself regularly avoiding certain tasks or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I avoid them regularly… not in a tidy way, anyway. I can get sort of… sidetracked from things that feel unpleasant, like forms or phone calls, but then sometimes I’ll decide I’m absolutely going to reorganize an entire shelf of inks instead, because that feels more “useful,” even if it’s not the thing I was meant to do.\n\nSo… maybe? But I’m not sure it’s anxiety exactly. It’s more that certain tasks feel heavy or irritating, and then my mind goes somewhere else. Like I’ll mean to answer one email and suddenly I’m thinking about the light in the studio window and whether the curtains are wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your current situation is impacting your ability to maintain your relationships or perform your job to the best of your ability?\n\nPatient: Yes… it is, unfortunately. I keep wanting to explain it away as “just a busy patch,” because with illustration there are always odd stretches where everything piles up, especially if the paper stock is wrong or someone changes a brief at the last minute—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is my deadlines have slipped. People I work with have had to check in on me, which feels awful because I’m usually the one who knows where everything is. And I’ve overcommitted myself, said yes to too much, spent money I should have kept back for the studio… and now the mortgage is at risk. So yes, it’s affecting work and relationships in a real way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
